## GHSA-jjq7-m736-w977 · openc3

2026-09-23 · Ruby · **CRITICAL** 9.9 · Pattern: `MISSING_AUTHZ→RESOURCE`

### Root cause

The system allowed authenticated non-admin users to write to specific configuration overlay paths (targets_modified/TARGET/cmd_tlm/) which were later loaded and executed as code (via ERB rendering and GENERIC_*_CONVERSION evaluation) by PacketConfig. This bypasses intended authorization checks for code execution.

### Impact

An authenticated attacker could inject and execute arbitrary code on the server, leading to full system compromise and potentially impacting the underlying infrastructure.

### Key diff

```
--- a/openc3-cosmos-cmd-tlm-api/app/controllers/storage_controller.rb
+++ b/openc3-cosmos-cmd-tlm-api/app/controllers/storage_controller.rb
@@ -429,8 +429,10 @@ def get_upload_presigned_request
       end
     end
 
-    # Anywhere other than config/SCOPE/targets_modified or config/SCOPE/tmp requires admin
-    if !(params[:bucket] == 'OPENC3_CONFIG_BUCKET' && (key_split[1] == 'targets_modified' || key_split[1] == 'tmp'))
+    # Non-admins may only write the user-writable overlay (config/SCOPE/targets_modified
+    # or config/SCOPE/tmp), and never the cmd_tlm overlay, which PacketConfig ERB-renders
+    # and whose GENERIC_*_CONVERSION blocks it evaluates as code. Everything else is admin.
+    unless non_admin_config_overlay_write?(params[:bucket], path)
       return unless authorization('admin')
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a new authorization check, `non_admin_config_overlay_write?`, to explicitly prevent non-admin users from writing to the `cmd_tlm` overlay path within the `targets_modified` directory. Additionally, a new `original: true` parameter is added to the `TargetFile.body` method, ensuring that code execution paths only load files from the read-only plugin-installed `targets/` tree, ignoring user-writable overlays.

## GHSA-rr49-f9g6-c9r5 · plone.app.portlets

2026-09-23 · Python · **CRITICAL** 9.9 · Pattern: `UNSANITIZED_INPUT→TEMPLATE`

### Root cause

The application allowed user-controlled input (template and macro names) to be directly used in a TALES (TAL Expression Syntax) path expression without sufficient validation. This enabled an attacker to inject TALES metacharacters, transforming a simple path traversal into an arbitrary TALES expression, which could then execute Python code.

### Impact

An attacker could achieve arbitrary remote code execution on the server, leading to full compromise of the application and potentially the underlying system.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/plone/app/portlets/portlets/classic.py
+++ b/src/plone/app/portlets/portlets/classic.py
@@ -13,6 +30,7 @@ class IClassicPortlet(IPortletDataProvider):
         title=_("Template"),
         description=_("The template containing the portlet."),
         required=True,
+        constraint=_valid_name,
     )
 
     macro = schema.ASCIILine(
@@ -22,6 +40,7 @@ class IClassicPortlet(IPortletDataProvider):
         ),
         default="portlet",
         required=False,
+        constraint=_valid_name,
     )
 
 
@@ -48,6 +67,20 @@ def use_macro(self):
         return bool(self.data.macro)
 
     def path_expression(self):
+        # Defense in depth: validate again at render time so assignments
+        # created programmatically (e.g. via GenericSetup import) that bypass
+        # the form field constraint are still rejected. Raises on illegal names.
+        _valid_name(self.data.template)
+        _valid_name(self.data.macro)
         expr = "context/%s" % self.data.template
         if self.use_macro():
             expr += "/macros/%s" % self.data.macro
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a strict regular expression validator for the 'template' and 'macro' fields. This validator ensures that only alphanumeric characters, underscores, at signs, periods, hyphens, and forward slashes are allowed, effectively preventing the injection of TALES metacharacters like ':' or '|'. The validation is applied both at the schema level and as a defense-in-depth measure at render time.

## GHSA-5f42-97gr-vfhq · io.moquette:moquette-broker

2026-09-23 · Java · **CRITICAL** 9.6 · Pattern: `INSECURE_DEFAULT→CONFIG`

### Root cause

The Moquette broker would silently fail to load configured authenticator or authorizator policies if the specified class was not found or could not be instantiated. Instead of preventing startup, it would proceed with default (potentially insecure) policies, leading to authorization bypasses and other issues.

### Impact

An attacker could bypass authorization checks, potentially gaining unauthorized access to resources or performing actions they shouldn't be allowed to. This could lead to cross-tenant authorization bypasses and other security vulnerabilities.

### Key diff

```
--- a/broker/src/main/java/io/moquette/broker/Server.java
+++ b/broker/src/main/java/io/moquette/broker/Server.java
@@ -480,6 +480,11 @@ private IAuthorizatorPolicy initializeAuthorizatorPolicy(IAuthorizatorPolicy aut
         String authorizatorClassName = props.getProperty(IConfig.AUTHORIZATOR_CLASS_NAME, "");
         if (authorizatorPolicy == null && !authorizatorClassName.isEmpty()) {
             authorizatorPolicy = loadClass(authorizatorClassName, IAuthorizatorPolicy.class, IConfig.class, props);
+            if (authorizatorPolicy == null) {
+                throw new IllegalArgumentException("Unable to load configured " + IConfig.AUTHORIZATOR_CLASS_NAME
+                    + " '" + authorizatorClassName + "'; rejecting to start. "
+                    + "Check the class name and classpath (see the WARN log above for the cause).");
+            }
         }
 
         if (authorizatorPolicy == null) {
```

### Fix summary

The patch modifies the server initialization logic to explicitly check if the configured authenticator and authorizator policies are successfully loaded. If loading fails, the server now throws an IllegalArgumentException and refuses to start, preventing it from operating with potentially insecure default policies.

## GHSA-x5c9-v98j-722r · 9router

2026-09-23 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 8.3 · Pattern: `MISSING_AUTH→ENDPOINT`

### Root cause

The application incorrectly determined if a request was local by only checking the TCP socket's remote address. When deployed behind a reverse proxy, the proxy's loopback address was considered 'local', allowing unauthenticated access to APIs intended for local-only use, even if the actual client was remote.

### Impact

An attacker could bypass authentication for /v1 APIs by sending requests through a reverse proxy, gaining unauthorized access to sensitive functionalities or data that should only be accessible from the local machine.

### Key diff

```
const ip = req.socket && req.socket.remoteAddress ? req.socket.remoteAddress : "";
+    const viaProxy = !!(req.headers["x-forwarded-for"] || req.headers["x-real-ip"]);
     delete req.headers["x-9r-real-ip"];
-    delete req.headers["x-forwarded-for"];
+    delete req.headers["x-forwarded-for"];
+    delete req.headers["x-9r-via-proxy"];
     req.headers["x-9r-real-ip"] = ip;
+    if (viaProxy) req.headers["x-9r-via-proxy"] = "1";
     return handler(req, res);
...
 export function isLocalRequest(request) {
+  if (request.headers.get("x-9r-via-proxy")) return false;
   const realIp = request.headers.get("x-9r-real-ip");
   if (realIp) {
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a new header, 'x-9r-via-proxy', which is set if 'x-forwarded-for' or 'x-real-ip' headers are present, indicating a request came via a reverse proxy. The 'isLocalRequest' function now explicitly checks for this new header and immediately returns false if it's present, preventing reverse proxy requests from being considered local.

> ⚡ **Pattern match — 70 occurrences**
> Languages: Python, JavaScript, Go, PHP, C#, RUST, Java

## GHSA-9rg8-2wvr-fgjh · verbb/formie

2026-09-23 · PHP · **HIGH** 7.7 · Pattern: `MISSING_AUTHZ→RESOURCE`

### Root cause

The `actionGetResendModalContent` endpoint, which renders a modal containing sensitive submission data, lacked proper authorization checks. Any authenticated user could access this endpoint and view personal identifiable information (PII) from form submissions, even if they didn't have the necessary permissions.

### Impact

An attacker with a low-privileged authenticated account could access and view sensitive personal identifiable information (PII) from form submissions, leading to unauthorized data disclosure.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/controllers/SentNotificationsController.php
+++ b/src/controllers/SentNotificationsController.php
@@ -61,6 +62,7 @@ public function actionEdit(int $sentNotificationId = null, SentNotification $sen
     public function actionGetResendModalContent(): Response
     {
         $this->requireAcceptsJson();
+        $this->requirePermission('formie-viewSentNotifications');
 
         $request = $this->request;
         $view = $this->getView();
```

### Fix summary

The patch adds a `requirePermission('formie-viewSentNotifications')` check to the `actionGetResendModalContent` method. This ensures that only users with the specific 'formie-viewSentNotifications' permission can access the resend modal content, thereby enforcing proper authorization.

## GHSA-gvf2-2rh5-mpgf · @openc3/vue-common

2026-09-23 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 7.6 · Pattern: `UNSANITIZED_INPUT→XSS`

### Root cause

The vulnerability existed because the application allowed users to save arbitrary HTML content, including script tags, within the 'BUTTON' widget on the Telemetry screen. This content was not properly sanitized before being rendered in the browser, leading to the execution of malicious scripts.

### Impact

An attacker could inject malicious scripts into the application, which would then be executed in the browsers of other users who view the affected Telemetry screen. This could lead to session hijacking, defacement, data theft, or further client-side attacks.

### Key diff

```
--- a/openc3-cosmos-init/plugins/packages/openc3-tool-base/public/js/vue.global.prod-3.5.39.min.js
+++ /dev/null
deleted file mode 100644
index 1a44fdb69c..0000000000
```

### Fix summary

The patch removes the `vue.global.prod-3.5.39.min.js` file, which likely contained the vulnerable component or an outdated version of the Vue framework that was being used to render unsanitized user input. The removal suggests a broader refactoring or update to a secure rendering mechanism.

## GHSA-9643-4qgh-g8mx · elysia

2026-09-23 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 7.5 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The application processed form data by iterating through keys and then calling `form.getAll(key)` for each key. If an attacker sent a form with many duplicate keys (e.g., `a=1&a=2&a=3...`), `form.getAll(key)` would repeatedly iterate through the entire form data for each key, leading to quadratic time complexity (O(N^2)) instead of linear (O(N)).

### Impact

An attacker could send a specially crafted form data payload with a large number of duplicate keys, causing the server to spend excessive CPU time processing the request. This could lead to a denial of service, making the application unresponsive to legitimate users.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/adapter/web-standard/index.ts
+++ b/src/adapter/web-standard/index.ts
@@ -58,9 +58,10 @@ export const WebStandardAdapter: ElysiaAdapter = {
 					`const m=k.match(/^(.+)\[(\d+)\]$/);` +
 					`return m?{name:m[1],index:parseInt(m[2],10)}:null` +
 					`}\n` +
-					`for(const key of form.keys()){` +
+					`const grouped=new Map()\n` +
+					`form.forEach((v,k)=>{const l=grouped.get(k);if(l)l.push(v);else grouped.set(k,[v])})\n` +
+					`for(const [key,value] of grouped){` +
 					`if(c.body[key])continue\n` +
-					`const value=form.getAll(key)\n` +
 					`let finalValue\n` +
```

### Fix summary

The patch changes how form data is processed. Instead of repeatedly calling `form.getAll(key)`, it first groups all values by their keys into a `Map` in a single pass. Then, it iterates through this `grouped` map, ensuring that each key's values are retrieved efficiently, reducing the complexity from quadratic to linear.

## GHSA-r2xf-8xr9-62gw · org.jline:jline-builtins

2026-09-23 · Java · **HIGH** 7.5 · Pattern: `UNSANITIZED_INPUT→REGEX`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from the JLine library's built-in 'grep' command, which automatically wrapped user-provided search patterns with '.*'. This behavior, combined with the use of a backtracking regex engine, allowed for the creation of 'catastrophic backtracking' regular expressions. When a specially crafted, complex regex pattern was applied to a non-matching input, the regex engine would consume excessive CPU resources, leading to a denial of service.

### Impact

An attacker could craft a malicious regular expression that, when processed by the JLine 'grep' command, would cause the application to become unresponsive or crash due to excessive CPU consumption, effectively denying service to legitimate users.

### Key diff

```
--- /dev/null
+++ b/terminal/src/main/java/org/jline/utils/SafeRegex.java
@@ -0,0 +1,218 @@
+public final class SafeRegex {
+    private static final long DEFAULT_TIMEOUT_MS = 1500;
+    private static final int CHECK_INTERVAL = 1024;
+    public static Matcher matcher(Pattern pattern, CharSequence input, long timeoutMs) {
+        long timeoutNanos = TimeUnit.MILLISECONDS.toNanos(timeoutMs);
+        return pattern.matcher(new TimeoutCharSequence(input, timeoutNanos));
+    }
+private static final class TimeoutCharSequence implements CharSequence {
+        private final CharSequence inner;
+        private final long timeoutNanos;
+        private final long[] sharedDeadline;
+        private int calls;
+        public char charAt(int index) {
+            if (++calls % CHECK_INTERVAL == 0) {
+                long now = System.nanoTime();
+                if (sharedDeadline[0] == 0) {
+                    sharedDeadl
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a new `SafeRegex` utility class that wraps `CharSequence` inputs with a `TimeoutCharSequence`. This wrapper enforces a wall-clock deadline during regex matching. If a regex operation exceeds a predefined timeout (defaulting to 1500ms), a `RegexTimeoutException` is thrown, preventing catastrophic backtracking from consuming infinite resources.

## GHSA-w4cm-gvhj-cgw6 · org.typelevel:jawn-parser_3

2026-09-23 · Java · **HIGH** 7.5 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The AsyncParser in Jawn could suffer from quadratic parsing effort when processing JSON data in small, incremental chunks. This was due to repeated rescanning of already processed data, leading to O(n^2) complexity for certain inputs, rather than the expected O(n).

### Impact

An attacker could provide specially crafted JSON input in small chunks, causing the parser to consume excessive CPU resources and time, leading to a denial of service for applications using the library.

### Key diff

```
--- a/parser/shared/src/main/scala/jawn/AsyncParser.scala
+++ b/parser/shared/src/main/scala/jawn/AsyncParser.scala
@@ -202,11 +208,20 @@ final class AsyncParser[J] protected[jawn] (
   @inline final private[this] def ASYNC_POSTVAL = -2
   @inline final private[this] def ASYNC_PREVAL = -1
 
+  private[this] def resumePos: Int = if (state > 0) curr else offset
+
   protected[jawn] def churn()(implicit facade: Facade[J]): Either[ParseException, collection.Seq[J]] = {
 
     // accumulates json values
     val results = mutable.ArrayBuffer.empty[J]
 
+    // rescanSize is the number of bytes scanned and discarded in the
+    // middle of a token.  Defer until this many additional bytes have
+    // arrived so we do O(n) work instead of O(n^2) if fed tiny
+    // chunks.
+    if (!done && (len - resumePos) - rescanSize < rescanSize)
+      return Right(results)
+
     // we rely on exceptions to tell us when we run out of data
     try {
       while (true)
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a `rescanSize` variable to track the amount of data that has been scanned and discarded in the middle of a token. The parser now defers processing until a sufficient number of additional bytes have arrived, preventing repeated rescans and ensuring O(n) parsing performance.

## GHSA-wmw4-mw6x-6vfm · @fecommunity/reactpress

2026-09-23 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 7.5 · Pattern: `UNSANITIZED_INPUT→SQL`

### Root cause

The application dynamically constructed TypeORM queries using user-supplied column names without proper validation or whitelisting. This allowed an attacker to inject arbitrary SQL into the column name part of the query, leading to SQL injection.

### Impact

An attacker could execute arbitrary SQL queries against the database, potentially leading to data exfiltration, modification, or deletion, and could bypass authentication or gain unauthorized access to sensitive information.

### Key diff

```
--- a/server/src/modules/file/file.service.ts
+++ b/server/src/modules/file/file.service.ts
@@ -72,11 +74,10 @@ export class FileService {
       query.skip((+page - 1) * +pageSize);
       query.take(+pageSize);
 
-      if (otherParams) {
-        Object.keys(otherParams).forEach((key) => {
-          query.andWhere(`file.${key} LIKE :${key}`).setParameter(`${key}`, `%${otherParams[key]}%`);
-        });
-      }
+      const filtered = filterByWhitelist('file', queryParams);
+      Object.keys(filtered).forEach((key) => {
+        query.andWhere(`file.${key} LIKE :${key}`).setParameter(`${key}`, `%${filtered[key]}%`);
+      });
     }
 
     return query.getManyAndCount();
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a `query-whitelist.util.ts` file that defines allowed column names for different entities. It then modifies services (e.g., `FileService`, `CommentService`) to use the `filterByWhitelist` function, ensuring that only whitelisted column names are used in TypeORM queries, preventing dynamic column name injection.

## GHSA-cmhj-wh2f-9cgx · 9router

2026-09-23 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 7.4 · Pattern: `SSRF→INTERNAL_ACCESS`

### Root cause

The application performed a DNS lookup to validate if a hostname resolved to a public IP address, but then used the original hostname for the actual HTTP fetch. An attacker could exploit DNS rebinding by having the DNS server initially return a public IP, and then return a private IP on a subsequent lookup (or during the connection phase if the DNS TTL expired), allowing the application to connect to internal services.

### Impact

An attacker could bypass the SSRF protection and make the server fetch resources from internal network services, potentially leading to information disclosure, unauthorized access, or further attacks on the internal infrastructure.

### Key diff

```
--- a/open-sse/translator/concerns/image.js
+++ b/open-sse/translator/concerns/image.js
-  if (!(await assertPublicHost(url.hostname))) return null;
+  const pinnedIps = await resolvePinnedIps(url.hostname);
+  if (!pinnedIps) return null;
 
   const controller = new AbortController();
   const timeout = signal ? null : setTimeout(() => controller.abort(), timeoutMs);
   const fetchSignal = signal || controller.signal;
 
+  // Pin connect to the validated IP so no second DNS resolution can rebind (TOCTOU fix).
+  const dispatcher = new Agent({
+    connect: { lookup: (_h, _o, cb) => cb(null, [{ address: pinnedIps[0].address, family: pinnedIps[0].family }]) },
+  });
+
   try {
     // redirect:"manual" prevents a public URL redirecting to a private one (SSRF bypass).
-    const response = await fetch(imageUrl, { signal: fetchSignal, redirect: "manual" });
+    const response = await fetch(imageUrl, { signal: fetchSignal, redirect: "manual", dispatcher });
```

### Fix summary

The patch modifies the image fetching logic to resolve the hostname to IP addresses once and then 'pins' the connection to one of the validated public IP addresses using an `undici.Agent`. This prevents a second DNS resolution during the connection phase, effectively mitigating DNS rebinding attacks.

## GHSA-ph9c-7hw9-vhhw · org.jline:jline-builtins

2026-09-23 · Java · **MODERATE** 6.5 · Pattern: `UNSANITIZED_INPUT→REGEX`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from the use of Java's backtracking regular expression engine without any safeguards. Certain complex regex patterns, especially those with nested quantifiers, could be exploited with specially crafted input strings to cause the regex engine to consume exponential time, leading to a denial of service.

### Impact

An attacker could provide a malicious regex pattern or input string that causes the application to spend an inordinate amount of time processing the regex, leading to high CPU utilization and making the application unresponsive or crashing it, thereby achieving a denial of service.

### Key diff

```
--- /dev/null
+++ b/terminal/src/main/java/org/jline/utils/SafeRegex.java
@@ -0,0 +1,218 @@
+public final class SafeRegex {
+    private static final long DEFAULT_TIMEOUT_MS = 1500;
+    private static final int CHECK_INTERVAL = 1024;
+    public static Matcher matcher(Pattern pattern, CharSequence input, long timeoutMs) {
+        long timeoutNanos = TimeUnit.MILLISECONDS.toNanos(timeoutMs);
+        return pattern.matcher(new TimeoutCharSequence(input, timeoutNanos));
+    }
+private static final class TimeoutCharSequence implements CharSequence {
+        private final CharSequence inner;
+        private final long timeoutNanos;
+        private final long[] sharedDeadline;
+        private int calls;
+        @Override
+        public char charAt(int index) {

```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a new utility class, `SafeRegex`, which wraps the input `CharSequence` with a `TimeoutCharSequence`. This wrapper enforces a wall-clock deadline during regex matching by checking `System.nanoTime()` periodically. If the matching operation exceeds the predefined timeout, a `RegexTimeoutException` is thrown, preventing catastrophic backtracking from consuming excessive resources.

## GHSA-6mwv-4mrm-5p3m · 9router

2026-09-23 · JavaScript · **MODERATE** 6.4 · Pattern: `SSRF→INTERNAL_ACCESS`

### Root cause

The application dynamically constructed URLs for AWS OIDC and CodeWhisperer endpoints by directly interpolating user-controlled 'region' parameters without proper validation. This allowed an authenticated attacker to inject arbitrary hostnames into the URL.

### Impact

An authenticated attacker could achieve Server-Side Request Forgery (SSRF) to make the server send requests to arbitrary internal or external hosts. The vulnerability was exacerbated by the fact that the Authorization header, potentially containing sensitive tokens, was forwarded to the attacker-controlled endpoint.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/lib/oauth/services/kiro.js
+++ b/src/lib/oauth/services/kiro.js
@@ -17,6 +17,7 @@ export class KiroService {
    * Returns clientId and clientSecret for device code flow
    */
   async registerClient(region = "us-east-1") {
+    assertValidAwsRegion(region);
     const endpoint = `https://oidc.${region}.amazonaws.com/client/register`;
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a new utility function, `assertValidAwsRegion`, which uses a regular expression (`AWS_REGION_PATTERN`) to validate that the provided region string conforms to the expected AWS region format (e.g., 'us-east-1'). This validation is applied to all instances where the 'region' parameter is used to construct a URL for AWS services.

## GHSA-m8r3-22v6-g877 · redaxo/source

2026-09-23 · PHP · **MODERATE** 6.4 · Pattern: `CSRF→STATE_CHANGE`

### Root cause

The `api_package_update.php` endpoint, responsible for updating packages, lacked Cross-Site Request Forgery (CSRF) protection. This meant that the application did not verify if the request originated from a legitimate user session, allowing external sites to trigger this action.

### Impact

An attacker could craft a malicious webpage that, when visited by an authenticated administrator, would force the administrator's browser to send a request to update an addon on the REDAXO installation. This could lead to an attacker forcing the installation of a malicious or unwanted addon.

### Key diff

```
--- a/redaxo/src/addons/install/lib/api/api_package_update.php
+++ b/redaxo/src/addons/install/lib/api/api_package_update.php
@@ -37,4 +37,9 @@ public function execute()
         }
         return new rex_api_result($success, $message);
     }
+
+    protected function requiresCsrfProtection()
+    {
+        return true;
+    }
 }
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a `requiresCsrfProtection()` method within the `api_package_update` class, which returns `true`. This explicitly enables CSRF protection for the package update action, ensuring that all requests to this endpoint are checked for a valid CSRF token.

## GHSA-5q95-hrpc-m3w3 · org.jline:jline-reader

2026-09-23 · Java · **MODERATE** 5.5 · Pattern: `UNSANITIZED_INPUT→REGEX`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from the use of Java's `java.util.regex` engine with user-controlled regular expressions, specifically in the `HISTORY_IGNORE` configuration. Certain regex patterns with nested quantifiers could lead to catastrophic backtracking, causing the regex engine to take exponential time on crafted input.

### Impact

An attacker could supply a specially crafted regular expression that would cause the application to consume excessive CPU resources, leading to a denial of service (DoS) for the application or system.

### Key diff

```
--- /dev/null
+++ terminal/src/main/java/org/jline/utils/SafeRegex.java
@@ -0,0 +1,218 @@
+public final class SafeRegex {
+    private static final long DEFAULT_TIMEOUT_MS = 1500;
+    private static final int CHECK_INTERVAL = 1024;
+    public static Matcher matcher(Pattern pattern, CharSequence input, long timeoutMs) {
+        long timeoutNanos = TimeUnit.MILLISECONDS.toNanos(timeoutMs);
+        return pattern.matcher(new TimeoutCharSequence(input, timeoutNanos));
+    }
+    private static final class TimeoutCharSequence implements CharSequence {
+        private final CharSequence inner;
+        private final long timeoutNanos;
+        private final long[] sharedDeadline;
+        private int calls;
+        public char charAt(int index) {
+            if (++calls % CHECK_INTERVAL == 0) {
+                long now = System.nanoTime();
+                if (sharedDeadline[0] == 0) {
+                    sharedDeadl
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a new `SafeRegex` utility class that wraps regex matching operations with a timeout mechanism. It uses a `TimeoutCharSequence` which monitors the number of `charAt` calls during regex matching and throws a `RegexTimeoutException` if a predefined deadline is exceeded, thus preventing catastrophic backtracking from consuming infinite resources.

## GHSA-5mmc-8pc9-wggg · wp-graphql/wp-graphql

2026-09-23 · PHP · **MODERATE** 5.4 · Pattern: `PRIVILEGE_ESCALATION→ROLE`

### Root cause

The WPGraphQL plugin did not properly enforce WordPress's object-level and publishing capabilities when updating posts via the GraphQL API. Specifically, it failed to check if a user had the `edit_post` capability for a specific post or the `publish_posts` capability when attempting to change a post's status to published, allowing users with lower privileges (like Contributors) to bypass these checks.

### Impact

An attacker with Contributor-level access could publish their own posts or modify already published posts, including those by other authors, without the necessary permissions, leading to unauthorized content changes or publication.

### Key diff

```
--- a/plugins/wp-graphql/src/Mutation/PostObjectUpdate.php
+++ b/plugins/wp-graphql/src/Mutation/PostObjectUpdate.php
@@ -114,6 +114,18 @@ public static function mutate_and_get_payload( $post_type_object, $mutation_name
 				throw new UserError( esc_html( sprintf( __( 'Sorry, you are not allowed to update another author\'s %1$s', 'wp-graphql' ), $post_type_object->graphql_single_name ) ) );
 			}
 
+			if ( ! isset( $post_type_object->cap->edit_post ) || ! current_user_can( $post_type_object->cap->edit_post, $post_id ) ) {
+				throw new UserError( esc_html( sprintf( __( 'Sorry, you are not allowed to update this %1$s', 'wp-graphql' ), $post_type_object->graphql_single_name ) ) );
+			}
+
 			$author_id = absint( $existing_post->post_author );
 
 			/**
@@ -161,6 +173,21 @@ public static function mutate_and_get_payload( $post_type_object, $mutation_name
 			$post_args       = PostObjectMutation::prepare_post_object( $input, $post_type_object, $mutation_name );
 			$post_args['ID'] = $post_id;
 
+			if (
+				isset( $post_args['post_status'] ) &&
+				! in_array( $post_args['post_status'], [ 'draft', 'pending' ], true ) &&
+				( ! isset( $post_type_object->cap->publish_posts ) || ! current_user_can( $post_type_object->cap->publish_posts ) )
+			) {
+				throw new UserError( esc_html( sprintf( __( 'Sorry, you are not allowed to publish this %1$s', 'wp-graphql' ), $post_type_object->graphql_single_name ) ) );
+			}
+
 			$clean_args = wp_slash( (array) $post_args )
```

### Fix summary

The patch adds explicit capability checks within the `mutate_and_get_payload` function. It now verifies if the current user has the `edit_post` capability for the specific post being updated and the `publish_posts` capability if the requested post status is anything other than 'draft' or 'pending'.

## GHSA-gxrg-x694-283w · solspace/craft-freeform

2026-09-23 · PHP · **MODERATE** 5.3 · Pattern: `UNSANITIZED_INPUT→TEMPLATE`

### Root cause

The application was rendering user-supplied attribute values using the Twig templating engine without proper sanitization or exclusion for sensitive attributes. This allowed an attacker to inject Twig code into certain HTML tag attributes.

### Impact

An attacker could achieve limited code execution within the Twig context, potentially leading to information disclosure or other impacts depending on the available Twig functions and variables.

### Key diff

```
--- a/packages/plugin/src/Library/Attributes/Attributes.php
+++ b/packages/plugin/src/Library/Attributes/Attributes.php
@@ -297,7 +298,10 @@ public function toHtmlTagArray(?array $properties = null): array
             $replacements = [];
             foreach ($array as $key => $value) {
                 $key = $twig->render($key, $properties);
-                $value = !empty($value) ? $twig->render($value, $properties) : $value;
+                if (!empty($value) && !\in_array($key, self::EXCLUDED_TWIG_ATTRIBUTES, true)) {
+                    $value = $twig->render($value, $properties);
+                }
 
                 $replacements[$key] = $value;
             }
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a new constant `EXCLUDED_TWIG_ATTRIBUTES` which lists attributes like 'value', 'name', and 'id'. It then modifies the `toHtmlTagArray` method to prevent Twig rendering for values of these excluded attributes, thereby preventing template injection.

## GHSA-mf2p-wjp4-99pq · redaxo/source

2026-09-23 · PHP · **MODERATE** 4.8 · Pattern: `UNSANITIZED_INPUT→XSS`

### Root cause

The application failed to properly escape user-controlled input before rendering it in various HTML contexts. Specifically, filenames, article names, category names, media filenames, language names, and media manager type names were directly embedded into HTML without sanitization, allowing an attacker to inject malicious scripts.

### Impact

An attacker could inject arbitrary client-side script into the backend interface, leading to session hijacking, defacement, or redirection of legitimate users. This could compromise administrator accounts or other backend users.

### Key diff

```
--- a/redaxo/src/addons/mediapool/pages/sync.php
+++ b/redaxo/src/addons/mediapool/pages/sync.php
-            $e['label'] = '<label>' . $file . '</label>';
-            $e['field'] = '<input type="checkbox" name="sync_files[]" value="' . $file . '" />';
+            $e['label'] = '<label>' . rex_escape($file) . '</label>';
+            $e['field'] = '<input type="checkbox" name="sync_files[]" value="' . rex_escape($file) . '" />';
```

### Fix summary

The patch addresses the vulnerability by applying the `rex_escape()` function to all user-controlled strings before they are output into HTML. This ensures that special characters are properly encoded, preventing them from being interpreted as executable code.

## GHSA-w998-qmw9-mf4m · redaxo/source

2026-09-23 · PHP · **MODERATE** 4.8 · Pattern: `UNSANITIZED_INPUT→XSS`

### Root cause

The application was directly embedding user-controlled data, such as filenames and article/category names, into HTML output without proper escaping. This allowed an attacker to inject malicious scripts by crafting filenames or other data containing HTML or JavaScript.

### Impact

An attacker could inject arbitrary client-side scripts into the backend interface, leading to session hijacking, defacement, or redirection of legitimate users within the administrative panel.

### Key diff

```
--- a/redaxo/src/addons/mediapool/pages/sync.php
+++ b/redaxo/src/addons/mediapool/pages/sync.php
@@ -116,11 +116,11 @@
     foreach ($diffFiles as $file) {
         if (is_writable(rex_path::media($file))) {
             $e = [];
-            $e['label'] = '<label>' . $file . '</label>';
-            $e['field'] = '<input type="checkbox" name="sync_files[]" value="' . $file . '" />';
+            $e['label'] = '<label>' . rex_escape($file) . '</label>';
+            $e['field'] = '<input type="checkbox" name="sync_files[]" value="' . rex_escape($file) . '" />';
             $writable[] = $e;
         } else {
-            $notWritable[] = $file;
+            $notWritable[] = rex_escape($file);
         }
     }
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces the `rex_escape()` function to properly escape all user-controlled input before it is rendered in HTML contexts. This prevents malicious scripts from being executed when filenames, article names, or other dynamic content are displayed.

> ⚡ **Pattern match — 116 occurrences**

## GHSA-5vjj-2r48-q622 · zapros

2026-09-23 · Python · **MODERATE** 0.0 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The Zapros library did not limit the number of Content-Encoding layers it would attempt to decompress. An attacker could send a request with an excessively long and deeply nested Content-Encoding header, causing the server to spend an inordinate amount of time and resources decompressing the data.

### Impact

An attacker could trigger a denial of service by sending a specially crafted request, leading to resource exhaustion (CPU, memory) and making the server unresponsive to legitimate users.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/zapros/_models.py
+++ b/src/zapros/_models.py
@@ -771,6 +774,8 @@ def _get_content_decoder(self, chunk_size: int) -> ContentDecoder:
             return self._decoder
 
         encodings = [enc.strip().lower() for enc in encoding_header.split(",")]
+        if len(encodings) > MAX_DECODE_LAYERS:
+            raise DecodingError(f"Too many Content-Encoding layers ({len(encodings)}), maximum is {MAX_DECODE_LAYERS}")
         decoders: list[ContentDecoder] = []
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a maximum limit of 5 for Content-Encoding layers. If the number of encoding layers in the 'Content-Encoding' header exceeds this limit, a DecodingError is raised, preventing excessive decompression attempts.

## GHSA-6cp7-3m3c-5x5c · zapros

2026-09-23 · Python · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The streaming decoders (GZip, Deflate, Brotli, ZStandard) in `zapros` did not respect the requested chunk size when decompressing data. This allowed a malicious actor to send a single, highly compressed response chunk that, when decompressed, would allocate an unbounded amount of memory, leading to a 'decompression bomb' scenario.

### Impact

An attacker could cause the server or client processing the compressed data to consume excessive memory, leading to a denial of service (DoS) by crashing the application or exhausting system resources.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/zapros/_decoders.py
+++ b/src/zapros/_decoders.py
@@ -40,10 +40,13 @@
         return b""
 
 class GZipDecoder:
-    def __init__(self) -> None:
+    def __init__(self, chunk_size: int = CHUNK_SIZE) -> None:
+        self._chunk_size = chunk_size
         self._decompressor = zlib.decompressobj(zlib.MAX_WBITS | 16)
 
-    def decode(self, data: bytes) -> bytes:
+    def decode(self, data: bytes) -> Iterator[bytes]:
         try:
-            return self._decompressor.decompress(data)
+            while data:
+                out = self._decompressor.decompress(data, self._chunk_size)
+                data = self._decompressor.unconsumed_tail
+                if out:
+                    yield out
```

### Fix summary

The patch modifies the `decode` methods of all streaming decoders to explicitly pass and respect a `chunk_size` parameter to the underlying decompression libraries. This ensures that decompression operations yield data in bounded chunks, preventing unbounded memory allocation from a single compressed input.

## GHSA-7c7c-373r-gfjj · github.com/klever-io/klever-go

2026-09-23 · Go · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `UNSANITIZED_INPUT→NOSQL`

### Root cause

The vulnerability existed because the 'acc.Name' field, which is an on-chain account name, was directly interpolated into an Elasticsearch bulk update query using the 'painless' scripting language without proper escaping. This allowed an attacker to inject arbitrary script code into the query.

### Impact

An attacker could inject malicious scripts into the Elasticsearch update query, leading to data forgery within the explorer/indexer. This could manipulate account information, balances, or other critical data stored in Elasticsearch.

### Key diff

```
-\t\t\tacc.Name, acc.Nonce, acc.RootHash, acc.Balance, acc.FrozenBalance, acc.UnfrozenBalance, acc.Allowance, string(pData), acc.UpdatedAt))
+\t\t\tconverters.JsonEscape(acc.Name), acc.Nonce, acc.RootHash, acc.Balance, acc.FrozenBalance, acc.UnfrozenBalance, acc.Allowance, string(pData), acc.UpdatedAt))
```

### Fix summary

The patch addresses the vulnerability by applying 'converters.JsonEscape()' to the 'acc.Name' field before it is interpolated into the Elasticsearch 'painless' script. This ensures that any special characters in the account name are properly escaped, preventing script injection.

> ⚡ **Pattern match — 4 occurrences**
> Languages: Go, Python, JavaScript
> → GHSA-jfwg-rxf3-p7r9
> → GHSA-grp3-h8m8-45p7
> → GHSA-98xf-r82g-9mhx

🔗 [View commit](https://github.com/klever-io/klever-go/commit/f00366768b24be18fa7ae9de2e1905caa8b350af)

## GHSA-9wh6-9hq7-9688 · github.com/klever-io/klever-go

2026-09-23 · Go · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The system allowed the registration and update of validator nodes with unvalidated BLS public keys. An attacker could submit malformed BLS public keys, which would be accepted by the system without proper cryptographic validation.

### Impact

An attacker could register or update validators with invalid BLS public keys, potentially leading to consensus liveness issues and a denial of service for the network by disrupting the validator set's ability to reach consensus.

### Key diff

```
--- a/core/kapp/validators/validators.go
+++ b/core/kapp/validators/validators.go
@@ -251,6 +284,11 @@ func (v *validatorsKApp) Register(tc *transaction.CreateValidatorContract) (tran
 		return transaction.Transaction_ParameterInvalid, common.ErrInvalidValue
 	}
 
+	if err := v.validateBLSPublicKey(tc.GetConfig().GetBLSPublicKey()); err != nil {
+		ctx.Receipts().AddError(ctx.ContractID(), common.ErrFieldInvalidBLSKey, err.Error())
+		return transaction.Transaction_ParameterInvalid, err
+	}
+
 	// load Kapp Accoount
 	app, err := v.getKApp()
 	if err != nil:
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a `blsPublicKeyValidator` interface and implementation that performs cryptographic validation of BLS public keys. This validation is now enforced during validator registration and updates, ensuring that only well-formed, prime-order-subgroup G2 points are accepted as BLS public keys.

## GHSA-j43x-5hjq-rgxf · plug

2026-09-23 · Erlang · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The Plug framework's query parameter decoding logic did not limit the nesting depth of parameters. An attacker could craft a deeply nested query string, causing the application to spend excessive CPU time and memory in a quadratic-time operation during parsing, leading to resource exhaustion.

### Impact

An attacker could send a specially crafted HTTP request to a Plug application, causing it to consume excessive CPU and memory resources, leading to a denial of service for legitimate users.

### Key diff

```
--- a/lib/plug/conn/query.ex
+++ b/lib/plug/conn/query.ex
@@ -90,6 +90,8 @@ defmodule Plug.Conn.Query do
   @typedoc since: "1.16.0"
   @opaque decoder() :: map()
 
+  @max_nesting 32
+
   @doc """
   Decodes the given `query`.
 
@@ -211,7 +213,7 @@ defmodule Plug.Conn.Query do
         value = binary_part(key, 0, pos)
         pos = pos + 1
         rest = binary_part(key, pos, byte_size(key) - pos)
-        split_keys(rest, key, pos, pos, value, [{:root, value}])
+        split_keys(rest, key, pos, pos, value, [{:root, value}], 1)
       else
         _ -> [{:root, key}]
       end
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a maximum nesting limit (@max_nesting = 32) for query parameters. It adds a counter to the recursive `split_keys` and `assign_split` functions and calls `check_nesting!` to raise an `InvalidQueryError` if the limit is exceeded, preventing excessive recursion and resource consumption.
